On main and here alike every generic definition and its instance have the same verdict and value: the 23 rejected generic definitions are the ones whose hint-needing `N` is out of tail position, or a field of a constructor with no parameter (`MkW(N)`: typing hands that field no hint), or `J` computing (`GJr`, at `refl`). No program of the hunt had an instance run and a generic check that differed, before or after. Guards read what the bare `N` records (`Value.exact`-level: `==` does not compare a constructor's recorded parameters): an untyped `let`, `;` and match out of tail position at the hint `L(Nat)` records none (false on main, where it recorded `Nat`), in tail position `Nat`; an assigned `N` records `Nat` (false on main).
- **Cost.** As first landed (d862b8d8) the change cost +32% instructions retired on `Quicksort` and +52% on `HashMapTests` (computing every hint the typed check hands, read or not, cost twice main). Counted over `HashMapTests` and its library: 5,238 parameter types computed for embedded-value arguments of sealed programs' re-runs, whose hint becomes only a returned type nobody reads (main: 0), and 760 called bodies' codomains computed (main: 16) of which 186 are read (`Quicksort`: 10,545 such arguments; 3,993 codomains, 1,712 read; main 820). Amended the same day (the orchestrator: the cost is not accepted for a change that alters no verdict): an embedded value's hint only where its type is read, and the codomain at the read. Instructions retired, main before D124 (6ffed490) against the amended change, one core, interleaved: `Quicksort` 82.3G → 81.9G, `HashMapTests` 30.7G → 31.7G (+3%), `Arrays` 2.21G → 1.99G. **Corrected (checker8, 2026-11-22):** those figures summed `perf`'s `cpu_atom` line with `cpu_core`, and the `cpu_atom` line is noise (up to 10% from run to run). Re-measured on `cpu_core` alone, interleaved, three runs each (spread under 0.01%): main before D124 (12cd4f25, the same checker as 6ffed490) / the first landing (e5ff35cd) / the cost fix (e0b73bce) / main at 021ca7cd: `Quicksort` 75.21G / 99.12G (+31.8%) / 81.88G (+8.9%) / 81.88G (+8.9%); `HashMapTests` 28.12G / 42.68G (+51.8%) / 28.91G (+2.8%) / 28.92G (+2.8%); `Arrays` 1.992G / 2.123G (+6.6%) / 1.983G (−0.5%) / 1.983G (−0.5%). So the first landing's regression was real, and the fix recovered `HashMapTests` to +2.8% and `Arrays` to even, but not `Quicksort`, which stays +8.9% (it was reported as −0.5%). The amended change against the first landing: the dump (all 4,271 rows unlocated, the tour located), the sweep, `--probes` and the probe files identical; `--pushes` back to main's census (1,802 untyped borrow pushes with no stored type at `callFn`).
- **Evidence.**
  - A dump of every row's verdict, message, location and editor-note count, main (6ffed490) with the new block copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 146 blocks (4,271 rows), located over the 133 blocks of the tour (3,853 rows): identical, editor-note counts included.
  - New asserted block `Inductives.UntypedHints` (211 rows, every rejection's message asserted) and the record guards; mutation-tested (a wrong value row, `ITlC` at 4, and a flipped record guard each fail the build). Count 4,060 → 4,271.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every clause's count identical (103,912 states) but "a constructor value without its parameters, at a temporary", 3 → 1: the assigned `N` (`UntypedHints.ISbN`) and the arm of a match on a proof (`IPb`) now record their parameter; the one left is `J`'s (`IJs`). `--probes` identical; `--pushes` clean (no push with no stored type in a typed run), with 13 more untyped pushes of a borrow with no stored type at `callFn` (1,802 → 1,815), from the hint computations at more calls.
  - The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`.
  - RULES (the declarations paragraph's untyped inference, the history line) say so. The paper does not describe hints; unchanged.

## D125. A function whose body ends in `split` can be called: the untyped run of `split f in t` runs `t` (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
Decision (the orchestrator, from a fault the consistency proof's lane found and the orchestrator re-checked). The check of a body (`checkTailAt`) splits the goal at a `split`, but every evaluation (`evalCore`) rejected `split` ("split: only in tail position (where the goal is known)"), the untyped run of a called body included. So a data function ending in a split was accepted and every call of it the checker ran was rejected:
```
inductive B := T | F
def IsZ (n : Nat) : B := match n { Z => T, S(m) => F, }
def V (b : B) : Type := match b { T => Nat, F => Unit, }
def G (n : Nat) : V(IsZ(n)) := split IsZ { T => Z, F => (), }    -- accepted
def H : Nat := G(Z)                                                -- rejected: split: only in tail position
```
Every `split` in the examples was in a proof, whose calls are never run, which is why nothing showed it.
- **Rule.** The untyped run (a called body, a sealed program's re-run, the runtime) of `split f in t` runs `t` as a `let`'s tail runs its tail: same tail position, the hint only there (D124), the term's reading and flags `t`'s. A typed run that is not checking the end of a body still rejects it, having no goal to split. The split decides nothing a run needs: with D126 the term names the call it splits on and computes it.
- **The class, hunted before the fix** (`probes/split-callable/gen.py`, run on main as a file of plain `def`s): every form the check of a body accepts at the end of a data-valued function (a split in both forms, bare, under a `let`, under a `;`, in the arms of a match on a number, on an inductive value, on a known constructor, on a proof by type, on a value of a type with one constructor, on a stuck call generalised; a match on the call with no split; a call of a function ending in a split), each called at a known argument, at an unknown argument inside another function where the body runs and where the call closes off (then re-run at closed instances), and through a closure (a lambda ending in the form, called; a lambda calling it passed to a higher-order function; the function itself passed). On main every generic definition was accepted and every call of a split rejected (231 rows), and nothing else: the match on the call with no split was callable everywhere, `rewrite` cannot end a data function (its goal must be a proposition), and the `let`, `;` and match forms of the check are the evaluation's own. The class is the split alone.
- **Evidence.** See D126, landed with it.

## D126. The braces form of `split` writes the call it splits on: `split f(ā) { … }` is `split f in match f(ā) { … }` (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
**What to change in your code.** Write the arguments of the call the goal is stuck on:
```
split IsZ { T => …, F => … }                 -- before (D61): rejected now
split IsZ(n) { T => …, F => … }              -- now: notation for `split IsZ in match IsZ(n) { T => …, F => … }`
```
The old spelling is rejected with the message "split IsZ { … } names no call: write the call of IsZ the goal is stuck on, `split IsZ(a, …) { … }`, which is `split IsZ in match IsZ(a, …) { … }` (D126)". `split f in t` is unchanged. Write the call as the goal has it: its arguments' values must be the ones the goal's stuck call was made with (in each world the match computes the call again, and only a call written so is the generalised program, so only it is refined and picks the arm; any other is a second, unrelated split, and the proof fails, never wrongly succeeds). The call is run like any other: a call that takes a borrow leaves its content as a closed-off call does, and an argument passed by value is moved. So where a later term reads what the call borrows, write the call on a copy (`let s1 = clone(s); split BInsert(V, GetMut(…, &s1, …), k, v) { … }`), and pass `clone(v)` where `v` is read later.
**Why** (the orchestrator, ruled 2026-11-22). D61's braces form named only the function: resolution made it `.prim "splitArms" [f, let ⋄split = (); match ⋄split { arms }]`, and the check bound `⋄split` to the result it found in the goal's value (`splitTarget`), ignoring the placeholder. A run has no goal with a stuck call left in it, so nothing that has only the term can pick an arm, and with D125 a run of a split must run its term. Finding the call in the declared codomain's text at run time was considered and rejected: the call need not be in that text (it can arise only after a type function is unfolded), and it would be a second way of finding the scrutinee that must agree with the first. With the call written, the braces form is notation over `split f in t` and one ordinary match; the primitive `splitArms` and its case in the check of a body are removed from the core (`Decide.declOf`'s case too). D61's design is otherwise unchanged: the split still finds its scrutinee in the goal (the first link with head `f`), and the written call only picks the arm.
- **Rewritten uses.** All 31 uses of the braces form, in `Examples/CaseSplits.lean` (4) and the hash-map case study (27), are rewritten and accepted, each with the same statement. Every call could be written as a term in scope: map-level splits on `BInsert`, `BRemove` and `BLookup` write the bucket as `GetMut(Bucket(V), Succ(n), &s1, Idx(k, n), IdxLt(k, n))` (on a copy `s1` of the slots wherever a later term reads them: 6 sites; a lookup after an insert or a remove reads the original slots); slot-level lookups bind the bucket (`let b0 = NthS(V, m, clone(t), i', hi)`, `let x0 = clone(x)`); `BFindLast`/`FindLast` take `clone(t)` or the `Rest(…)` list the goal has; the resize's `LtB` takes the length after the insert, `LtB(n, Len(V, clone(m2)))` under `split BInsert in`, or `LtB(n, Succ(len))` and `LtB(n, len)` per world. `InsertResultR`, whose two `LtB` arms were the same term, is now `split BInsert in split LtB in InsertResult(V, &mc, k, v)`. The case study's counted proofs go from 1,350 to 1,353 lines (21,825 to 22,506 tokens, +3.1%, by `notes/hashmap-count-ochr.py`); the paper's figure and text say so.
- **Evidence** (D125 and D126 together).
  - New asserted block `CaseSplits.SplitCallable` (279 rows): the lead's example in the braces form (`G`, `H`, `HV`: `H`'s value `Z` by `refl`) and in the `in` form (`Gi`, `Hi`, `HiV`); the old spelling (`GOld`, rejected, message asserted); and the hunt's table (21 shapes, 268 rows, generated by `probes/split-callable/gen.py`), every row accepted with its values by `refl`. Mutation-tested: a wrong value row (`SBrSV` at 2) and a wrong message guard each fail the build. Count 4,271 → 4,550.
  - A dump of every row's verdict, message, location and editor-note count, main (eda9e9cd) with the block copied in (in D61's spelling) and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 147 blocks (4,550 rows), 236 rows change, all in the new block: 235 calls of a split go from rejected ("split: only in tail position") to accepted, and `GOld` from accepted to rejected (the old spelling); outside it one message (`Splitting.WrongCtors`, still rejected: the matched temporary is now named after the call, `⋄IsZ(…)`, not `⋄split`). Located over the 134 blocks of the tour: the same, plus, in `CaseSplits.lean` only, byte offsets shifted by the rewritten spelling and the four rewritten `Splitting` rows' editor-note counts (each now evaluates its call). No accepted program is lost but the old spelling. The hash-map case study's rows are identical.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every clause's violation count identical (states 106,747 → 109,708, from the calls now run); `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean.
  - The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output, after `split-join/Hunt.lean`'s one braces row (`SPg`, rejected on main because its goal is `⊤`) is written `split SF(n) { … }` (rejected the same way).
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES ([Split-goal], the history line), the appendix's [T-Split-goal] paragraph, `lean/README.md`, the benchmark guide (`ochr/bench/common/ochr/GUIDE.md`) and the book (`docs/OCHR_BOOK.md`) give the new spelling. The Conclusion ends on page 25.

## D124, `J`. `J`'s transported term gets the hint `P(a)` whether `J` is stuck or computes, and the untyped run hands it where the term reads it (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
Decision (the orchestrator, ruling option (b) of the two put to it by checker7; the other, no hint on both paths, would have rejected the accepted `UntypedHints.GJs`). The typed check of `J(A, a, b, P, h, t)` checks `t` against `P(a)`; where `J` is stuck ([T-J-stuck], a block's one arm) it handed `t` the hint `P(a)`, where it computes ([J], typed) none, and the untyped run none. So of two twins that differ only in whether `J` computes, a bare constructor that needs its parameters (`N` of `L(A)`) was accepted where `J` is stuck (`GJs`) and rejected where it computes (`GJr`, "cannot infer the parameter A of N"), and an instance's run of the stuck one recorded no parameter where its check had one.
- **Rule.** Both typed paths hand `t` the hint `P(a)`. The untyped run (a called body, a sealed program's re-run) hands it where `t` reads it (`readsHint`, out of tail position: `t` itself a constructor of a parameterised type with its parameters not written) and only where `J` computes; it computes `P(a)` there, as the typed check does (`Machine.jMotiveAt`: `A` and `P` evaluated and `P` called at `a`'s value, on a private copy), before the transported term's entry. A `J` whose motive is a proposition (a proof, which always computes) likewise. The runtime's [J-run] is unchanged: in the erased program `J` is `t` and has no endpoints or motive to compute `P(a)` from, so a bare constructor there records no parameter, as before.
- **Twins** (in `Inductives.UntypedHints`, 211 → 244 rows): `J` stuck (`GJs…`, `h` unknown) and computing (`GJr…`, `refl`) with a bare constructor (`N`), one its fields determine (`C(clone(n), N)`), one with its parameter written (`N[Nat]`), and two terms that are not constructors (a call, `IdL(C[Nat](…))`, and `(let y = Z; N)`, which both paths reject, no hint reaching a `let`'s tail out of tail position); `J` as a `let`'s right-hand side and as a call's argument; a motive that depends on the endpoint (`λ(z : Nat) : Type => L(TN(z))`, stuck and computing, bare and written); an erased `J` (motive into `Prop`, a bare `PNil` of `PN(A) : Prop`), as a proof's body and as a statement in a data function's body. Each generic definition has its closed instance with the value by `refl`, and two guards read what a bare `N` records in an untyped run of a computing `J` (`Value.exact`-level: `Nat`, in tail position and not; false on main, where it recorded none).
- **Evidence.**
  - Dump, main (c9f14932) with the new rows copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 147 blocks (4,583 rows) and located over the 134 of the tour: 11 rows change, all from rejected to accepted, the `GJr`-like rows: `GJr`, `GJrLet`, `GJrA`, `GJdr` (a computing `J` transporting a bare `N`, at the end of a body, as a `let`'s right-hand side, as a call's argument, under a dependent motive) with their instances, and the erased `GJe`, `GJeD`, `IJeD`. No accepted program is lost; every stuck twin, written twin and non-constructor twin is unchanged.
  - Mutation-tested: a wrong value row (`IJrA` at 1) and a flipped record guard each fail the build. Count 4,550 → 4,583.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every clause's count identical (states 110,197 → 110,270) but two: "a constructor value without its parameters, at a temporary" 3 → 0 (`IJs`, `IJsLet`, `IJds`: the instance's run of a computing `J` now records the parameter), and "the goal holds an unknown" (known on main) 20,596 → 20,598 states, in the same 688 declarations (exit states of rows that are now accepted). `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - Cost, instructions retired (`cpu_core`, one core, interleaved, three runs each), main against this change: `Quicksort` 81.8825G → 81.8835G, `HashMapTests` 28.913G → 28.913G, `Arrays` 1.984G → 1.983G (run-to-run spread 0.5%): no change. (The `cpu_atom` line `perf` also prints is not this process's count and varies by 10% from run to run; D124's cost bullet, which summed the two lines, is corrected above.)
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES ([J]'s typing paragraph, the declarations paragraph's untyped inference, the history line) say so. The paper does not describe hints; unchanged.

## D124, an embedded value's type. The untyped run types an embedded value as the typed check does, where its type is read (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
Decision (the orchestrator: the last site of the proof lane's hint table, docs/31 D172 on `spine`: "an embedded value's type (typed: `valType`; untyped: the hint)", to be made to agree if the typed check's decision is available from syntax and declarations). An embedded value (`.val v`: a value inside a term the checker built, a sealed program's text, a closure's inlined capture, a closed-off `J`) has no type of its own in the term. The typed check types it by its kind (`valType`) and reads the hint only for a proof (a proposition's value, `⋆`) and for a loan whose borrow is gone; the untyped run returned the hint, whatever the value, so where its context read the type it could disagree: `0` embedded at the hint `L(Nat)` was typed `L(Nat)` in a run, `Nat` in the check, and a `let` binding an embedded number in a sealed program's re-run (no hint) stored no type where the check stores `Nat`.
- **The decision is available.** Which source types the value is decided by the value's kind, which is part of the term (the embedded value's head), and for a loan by whether its borrow is live, which both runs read from the same state; the typed check computes nothing the untyped run could not. So the untyped run follows it.
- **Rule.** Where its context reads its type (`want`: a `let`'s right-hand side, a borrow argument, a call's head), the untyped run types an embedded value by its kind (`valType?`: a number, an unknown, a constructor, a type, a closure, a sealed program, a live loan's content), else by the hint (a proof; a loan whose borrow is gone). This is the typed check's type wherever the typed check gives one; it differs only where the typed check fails (a proof at a hint that is not a proposition, a value whose type cannot be read), and there the untyped run, which does not check, keeps the hint. Where nothing reads the type it is not computed (the hint is returned, as before, unread).
- **Evidence.**
  - Guards in `Inductives.UntypedHints` (`runWant`: an untyped run whose context reads the type): `0` at the hint `L(Nat)` is typed `Nat`, and `N[Nat]` at the hint `L(Unit)` is typed `L(Nat)` (both false on main, where the hint was taken); a proof at `True` is typed `True` (true on main too). Mutation-tested: the first guard asserting main's answer fails the build.
  - Dump, main (2beffc60) with the guards copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 147 blocks (4,583 rows) and located over the 134 of the tour: identical, editor-note counts included. No verdict, message or location changes.
  - `lake exe sweep`, `--probes`, `--pushes`: identical, census included. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - Cost, instructions retired (`cpu_core`, one core, interleaved, two runs each): `Quicksort` 81.8836G → 81.8845G, `HashMapTests` 28.9133G → 28.9149G, `Arrays` 1.984G → 1.983G: no change.
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES (the declarations paragraph, the history line) say so. With this, every site of the audit's table has the untyped run follow the typed check's decision.

## D127. A borrow whose content is moved out is not read, and a partly moved place is not copied (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the consistency proof's lane, which pinned it: `origin/spine:ochr/core/lean/Ochr/Meaning/Steps/PlaceGuards.lean`). The read of a place (`Machine.readPlace`) took its borrow branch, moving the borrow, before the test every other value meets, that it holds no `⊥` (D53). So a borrow whose content was partly or wholly moved out could be moved to another name, passed to a call or assigned, provided whoever got it wrote the moved part back before anything read it; a callee that runs was checked on the actual state, which is what made it safe. With `inductive Box := MkBox(n : Nat)`:
```
def MB1 (a : Nat) (b : Nat) : Unit := (let x = (MkBox(a), b); let r = &x; let y = (*r).0; *r := (MkBox(Z), Z); ())               -- accepted, before and after
def MB2 (a : Nat) (b : Nat) : Unit := (let x = (MkBox(a), b); let r = &x; let y = (*r).0; let r2 = r; *r2 := (MkBox(Z), Z); ())  -- accepted before, rejected now
def MB3 (a : Nat) (b : Nat) : Unit := (let x = (MkBox(a), b); let r = &x; let y = (*r).0; ())                                     -- rejected, before and after (D53)
```
- **Why.** The consistency proof's model defines a function's meaning on the members of its parameter types, and a borrow whose content is moved out is not a member of `&T`. "This callee writes before it reads" is not in the callee's type, so the model cannot follow the run that checked it, and a consistency theorem must cover every accepted program. Rust rejects the same programs (nothing is moved out through a `&mut` there), so the bar "as good as Rust for borrowing" holds.
- **Rule.** A place holding a borrow whose content holds `⊥` is not read, in any mode of the read: "[Read] r is a borrow whose content is partly moved out ((⊥, σ1)): restore it before reading r (D127)", or, for a content moved out whole, "[Read] x is a borrow whose content was moved out: refill it through x (*x := …) before reading x (D127)". A partly moved place is not read by copy either (`clone`, an erased read): "[Read] x was partly moved out, and is not copied either (D127)". A place of a copy type is exempt, as for the moving read: it is never moved, and the `⊥` it can hold is inside a stuck proposition (D68).
- **What is lost.** A helper function can no longer be handed a borrow to refill. `Moves.TakeRefill`, `def TakeRefill (x : &Nat) : Unit := (let v = *x; Refill(x, v))` with `def Refill (x : &Nat) (v : Nat) : Unit := *x := v`, is now rejected: a deliberate loss, recorded as a `reject` row with its message. In the hunt, the rows that handed such a borrow on, each restoring the hole before any read: a `let` (`MB2`, and `LetF`, which restores one field through the new name), a call that runs whose callee writes the whole or the field (`CallFillP`, `CallFill0P`), re-pointing (`AsgP`, `r2 := r`), a parameter borrow handed on (`ParP`, `ParCallP`), a hole one level down (`NestP`); and copies of a partly moved place (`CloneDP`, `clone(*r)`; `CloneOP`, `clone(x)`; `EqOP`, `EqDP`, `Eq` of such copies, which `refl` proved equal).
- **What still works.** Refilling through the same borrow in the same function, an assignment, which is a write and not a read (`Moves.TakeAssign`: `let v = *x; *x := v`; `MovedBorrows.MB1`); refilling first and then handing the borrow on (`Moves.RefillFirst`; every `…W` twin of the hunt). Moving out of a place through a borrow is unchanged: only the borrow itself is not read while its content has a hole.
- **The way to get it back** is a type for a borrow that must be written before it is read (the user has explored one on other branches), so that the callee's type says what the run checked; not an exception to this rule.
- **The hunt, before the change** (`MovedBorrows`, generated from it): every route by which such a borrow could flow, each `…P` beside a `…W` twin that restores first: a `let`; a call that runs (the callee writes the whole, writes the field, reads the other field, reads the hole) and one that closes off; a result; an assignment's right-hand side; a capture; a match through the borrow (the moved field unused and used); a reborrow `&*r`; a stuck block's arm, out of tail position and in it; `clone(r)`, `clone(*r)`, an erased read. No accepted program on main let a callee or a later read observe the hole: every route that read it was already rejected (reading `⊥`, D53 at a borrow's end, at a call that closes off, at a result, [Borrow] at a reborrow, the capture rule). A constructor's field cannot hold a borrow.
- **Evidence.**
  - New asserted block `Borrows.MovedBorrows` (63 rows, every rejection's message asserted); `Moves` gains `TakeAssign` and `RefillFirst`, `TakeRefill` becomes `reject`, and a guard asserts its message and the seven below. Mutation-tested: a twin made `reject` and a wrong message each fail the build. Count 4,583 → 4,648.
  - A dump of every row, main (13dff276) with the new tests copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 148 blocks (4,648 rows) and located over the tour: 14 rows go from accepted to rejected, `Moves.TakeRefill` and the 13 hunt rows above; none from rejected to accepted; 21 rejected rows change their message, all in `Moves` and `MovedBorrows`. Seven of them are older rows, where the new test now fires before D53's: `Moves.ReturnMovedBorrow`, `RetMoved`, `B3` ("[D53] a returned borrow's content is partly moved out (⊥)") and `Moves.P1`, `P2`, `FullMove`, `TailMove` ("[D53] argument 1 of a call that closes off (an erased call, for `P2`) is a borrow whose content is partly moved out (⊥)"), each now "[Read] x is a borrow whose content was moved out: refill it through x (*x := …) before reading x (D127)" with its own variable. No row outside these two blocks changes; the case studies are identical.
  - `lake exe sweep`: no clause gains a violation; the known clauses lose the states of the rows that now stop earlier (states 111,668 → 111,486); `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES ([Read], the history line), the paper's figure of borrow rules (its caption) and its text on reading ("A borrow must be whole, with no `⊥` in its content, when it ends or is read (moved, returned, passed to a call)", which said "when it ends, is returned, or is passed to a call that closes off"), and the appendix's [Move] (premise: the content whole), [Read-err], [Clone] (the value whole) and the paragraph after them say so. The Conclusion ends on page 25.

## D129. The join of a match carries the places already moved at its entry as they are (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker8]
Decision (the orchestrator, from a fault the proof's machine lane found, `scratchpad/reports/machine10-JoinRegrain.lean`, re-run on main). The join of a match (D84, `Decide.jdMatch`, read by `Machine.staticJoin`) is the places its arms leave moved, each taken at the grain of the declared types: a move under the fields of a type with several constructors is a move of the value whole. It put the places already moved when the match starts (`acc`) through the grain too. So in an arm that had moved `x.n` (`x : B2`, two constructors), a nested match's join came back with the whole `x` moved, dropped it, and took `x.m` with it:
```
inductive L := LN | LC(h : Nat)
inductive B2 := A0(n : L, m : L) | A1
def f3 : L := (let x = A0(LN, LC(3)); match x { A0(n, m) => (let y = n; let w = match Z { Z => Z, S(j) => Z, }; m), A1 => LN, })   -- rejected: no such place x.m
def f (x : B2) (k : Nat) : L := match x { A0(n, m) => (let y = n; let w = match k { Z => Z, S(j) => Z, }; m), A1 => LN, }           -- accepted
def callF : L := f(A0(LN, LC(3)), Z)                                                                                                -- rejected: no such place x.m
```
A correct program was rejected, and a function accepted that cannot be called at a known argument (the class of D119 and D125). With a type of one constructor the grain keeps `x.n`, which is why it went unnoticed.
- **Rule.** The join takes at its grain only the places its arms add; a place already moved at the match's entry is carried as it is. It was moved exactly, on every path, before the match; the grain exists for what an arm moves under a constructor the generic call does not know.
- **The class, hunted** (`ReturnedBorrows.JoinEntry`, generated by `probes/join-entry/gen.py`, 185 rows): the outer part moved by a `let`, a call's argument, a constructor's field and a `;`'s tail; then a nested match on a known and an unknown number, on a known and an unknown value of an inductive, none, and one at the end of the arm; each a closed definition and a generic one, with values by `refl`; a part two levels down (`x.n.h`, through `n : L2` of two constructors). On main every definition with a nested match on a known scrutinee was rejected, and every instance of one on an unknown scrutinee; no accepted program was wrong (the fault rejects too much).
- **The reverse question: does the join now drop too little?** No: the entry places are exactly the places moved before the match, and every place an arm adds is still taken at its grain. Rows: an arm of the nested match that moves the rest, `m` (`VMv`, `VMvR`: the stuck block takes `x` whole, which holds a hole, rejected on every path, as before; `VMvK`, the arm not taken: its `x.m` is still taken at the grain, `x`, so the join drops `x` and a read of `m` after it is rejected, as before); an arm that refills the moved part (`VRf`: on an unknown scrutinee the block cannot borrow `⊥`, rejected, before with another message; `VRfK`, on a known one: the arm runs and refills `n`, the join drops it again since the other arm leaves it moved, and `m` is read after it, accepted now, rejected before; `VRfKn`, reading `n` after it: rejected, before and after).
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `ReturnedBorrows.JoinEntry` (185 rows, every rejection's message asserted), mutation-tested (a wrong value row and a wrong message each fail the build). Count 4,648 → 4,833.
  - Dump, main (eeb1347a) with the block copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated over all 149 blocks (4,833 rows) and located over the tour: 78 rows change, all in the new block: 74 from rejected to accepted, and 4 rejected rows change their message (`VRfI`, `VRfG`: "[Read] x was partly moved out (D53)" → "[Borrow] x.n was moved out (borrowing ⊥)"; `VRfKnI`, `VRfKnG`: "no such place x.n: its path does not exist in ⊥" → "[Read] x.n was moved out or its borrow ended (reading ⊥)"). Nothing accepted is lost; no other row changes.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every clause's violation count identical (states 114,187 → 115,064, from the rows now accepted); `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - No stability proof reads `jdMatch`; the build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES ([Join], the history line) and the appendix's [Join] paragraph say so. The Conclusion ends on page 25.

## D128. A type parameter of an inductive declaration is written as a sort (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from a fact the proof's data-model theorem rests on, found false by the proof lane: `origin/spine`, `Meaning/DataModel/Exists/Assembly.lean`, `VaryValFact` and `FieldHeads`, with the checked counterexample in `DataModel/Exists/FactsCE.lean`). The checker had two tests for "this parameter of an inductive declaration is a type": its written type is a sort (`IndDecl.paramIsType`, D71, which decides the type positions of `D(ā)` and the nestable parameters, D107), and its declared type reads as a sort (`telescope`, by which D55 types a field: a parameter heads a field type only if its declared type reads as a sort). They disagreed on
```
inductive TL (A : Type) (B : (let z = A; Type)) := MkTL(x : B)
```
which was accepted: `B` was a value parameter for nesting and a type for its field, and a field type read a value parameter that varies (its type reads `A`), which the data model's proof rules out (no field type reads a value parameter that varies; a parameter heading a field is declared at a written `Prop` or `Type`).
- **Rule.** A parameter whose declared type reads as a sort must be written as one; `TL` is rejected with "[D128] the parameter B of TL is declared at let z = A; Type, which reads as the sort Type without being written as one: a type parameter is written as a sort (write B : Type)". Decided in `Check.checkInd` right after the parameter telescope, from the written types and their readings, nothing run. So a parameter is a type for its fields exactly when it is a type parameter. The alternative, `paramIsType` reading the declared type, was refused: it would change which parameters are nestable.
- **The class, hunted** (`scratchpad/checker9/gen128.py`, kept as the asserted block below; each spelling at `Prop`, `Type` and `Type₁`, with a field `x : B` and with none; both tests printed by running `telescope` in the state after the block). Reads as the sort without being one: a `let` (`let z = A; S`, `let z = 0; S`, two `let`s), a `;` (`(); S`, `A; S`), a `let` naming the sort (`let T = S; T`), a match whose arms all return the sort (on a literal, which resolution writes as a `let`; on a `Nat`, `Bool` or `Word` parameter; through a `let`; with a `let` in an arm; returning a `let`-named sort). On main, with a field, the `let`, `;` and literal-match spellings at `Prop` and `Type` were accepted (`TL`'s class), those at `Type₁` rejected because the field is above `Type`, and the match on a parameter rejected at evaluation (the generic match is stuck, "#0 is not a type"); with no field, all were accepted. Reads as a value, not a sort, so a value parameter, as before: an ascription `(S : S')`, a call of a function returning the sort, a constant defined as the sort or by a hole, a type function `IdT(S)`, an earlier parameter `(U : S') (B : U)`, a match whose arms disagree (`Type`, `Prop`); accepted with no field, rejected by D55 as a field's type. A hole `?` as a parameter type is rejected (its type is not known). The mirror case, a parameter written as a sort whose reading is not one, cannot happen: `typeDecl` reads a written sort as itself.
- **Not changed:** a function's parameter written `(B : (let z = 0; Type))` is accepted, and so are its calls; the declared-type readers that substitute a function's type parameters (`typeFnDecl`, D80's `callDecl`) test the written sort, so they read such a parameter as a value, which reads less, never more.
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `Fording.WrittenSorts` (201 rows, generated by `probes/written-sorts/gen.py`): `TL` verbatim and nested in a list (`TLL`); each spelling that reads as a sort, at each sort, with a field and without, rejected, beside its twin written as the sort, whose verdict is unchanged; the spellings that read as a value as controls. Every rejection's message asserted. Mutation-tested: a flipped row and a wrong message each fail the build. Count 4,833 → 5,034.
  - A guard over the whole registry (`Registry.sortParamsNotWritten`): no accepted inductive declaration of any registered block has a parameter whose declared type reads as a sort and is not written as one, read in the state after the block and its library. It runs only the blocks with a parameter not written as a sort (30, about two seconds). Mutation-tested: with D128's check disabled and `TL` expected accepted, the guard fails.
  - Dump, main (400644c6) with the new tests copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated and located over all 5,034 rows: 80 rows change, all in the new block (57 from accepted to rejected, 23 rejected rows now rejected by D128). Nothing in the suite or the case studies changes, so no program is lost.
  - `lake exe sweep`: no clause gains a violation; the two known clauses ("a stored type holds an unknown", "the type of an unknown holds an unknown") lose the 30 declarations of the new block that are now rejected; `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - No stability proof reads `checkInd`; the build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES (§8, the history line) and the appendix's paragraph on inductive types say so.

## D130. The flag that defers a call's result type is consumed when an evaluation starts (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof lane's machine invariants, `origin/spine` `Meaning/Steps/LaterGuards.lean` and the lead's handoff there). When a `let` binds the result of a call in an untyped run, it sets `deferCallTy`, so that the call records its callee's Π-type and arguments (`callTyLater`) instead of computing its type, and the binding's type is computed only if it is read (D82). The checker's other one-shot flags (the tail flag `tailNext`, the handed-down reading `tailDecl`, the wanted type `wantTy`) are read by `evalAt` into the state the evaluation starts from and cleared; this one was cleared only by `evalCall`, so a flag left set would have been read by the next call the run made, whichever it was. In core terms, from a state with the flag set, `let y = (Z; Add(x, 0)); y` bound `y` with no type: the call in the `;`'s tail deferred its type, and the `let`, not bound to a call, read no record. No run of a program leaves the flag set, so no verdict depended on it; the proof's invariant had to carry "the flag is set only before a call".
- **Rule (no rule's text changes).** `evalAt` consumes the flag as it does the other one-shot flags: it reads it into the state the evaluation starts from (`deferHere`) and clears it, and `evalCall` reads the copy. `closeBlock`'s own call, whose type is not read, clears the copy with `wantHere`. The record is cleared when an evaluation starts too (in the same update, so it costs nothing), so a run that makes no call leaves none (the proof lane found stale records surviving such a run; harmless before, since the `let` cleared the record before its call, which it no longer needs to do).
- **The places, hunted.** The flag: set only by the untyped `let` of a call (`.letIn`), read only by `evalCall` (the evaluation of a call term, and `closeBlock`'s call of a stuck block, where `later` is false since its type is not wanted). The record: written by every `callFn` (when the Π-type is known, and again at its end, over the record of the calls the body made; not on the early return for a proof value), read only by the `let`, right after its right-hand side. A private copy (`inStatement`, `privately`) restores both with the state it saved: since the flag is consumed before an erased term's copy is taken, the copy can no longer bring it back. Found by the hunt and NOT changed here (to be D132): the record an erased call makes inside its statement's private copy is discarded with the copy, so a `let` of a call returning a type or a proof binds with neither a type nor a deferred type, and `useT(n) := (let T = TF(n); let z : T = 3; z)`, accepted, cannot be called (`useT(1)`: "cannot infer the type of the place T").
- **What the proof can rely on** (the lead's precision, 2026-11-22). (1) At every entry of `evalAt` the persistent flag is read into the per-evaluation copy (`deferHere`) and cleared, in one update, as `tailNext` is read into `curTail`; `evalCall` reads only the copy. So after any `evalAt` entry the persistent flag is clear, and a later sibling evaluation (the tail of a `;`, the tail of a `let`) never sees a flag an earlier part left. (2) The flag is set in one place only, the untyped `let` of a call (since D132, of a call the pre-pass does not erase), immediately before it evaluates its right-hand side by `eval`, which reaches `evalAt` through `located` and `noteValue` and nothing else. The one path on which the `let` sets it and `evalAt` is not entered is `eval` running out of fuel, which ends the declaration's check (undecided) and is never caught below the top level. A private copy restores the state it saved, but every copy is taken after the consumption (`evalAt` consumes before it runs an erased term on a copy), so none brings the flag back. The record `callTyLater` is cleared at the same entry, in the same update.
- **Evidence.**
  - Guards (`Units.lean`, `deferRun`: an untyped run from the state after `Nats` with the type wanted, the flag and a stale record set or not): a run with no call (`Z`) consumes the flag and leaves no record (before: both left set); a call whose evaluation starts with the flag set defers its type and leaves its record; `let y = (Z; Add(x, 0)); y` from a state with the flag set gives `Nat` (before: no type); a `let` of a call, with the flag set or not, still defers and gives `Nat` when read; and (added with D132) `let y = Z; Add(y, 0)` from a state with the flag set gives `Nat`, the `let`'s tail never seeing the flag (before D130 this held because the `let` cleared the flag after its right-hand side). The two that changed fail on main (run against main's checker).
  - Dump, main (5689f16e) with the guards copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated and located over all 5,034 rows: identical. `lake exe sweep`, `--probes`, `--pushes`: identical. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, one core, interleaved, three runs each, spread under 0.01%): `Quicksort` 81.923G → 81.936G (+0.02%), `HashMapTests` 28.931G → 28.939G (+0.03%), `Arrays` 1.9849G → 1.9854G (+0.02%).
  - No stability proof reads the flag; the build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES' history line says so; no rule's text, and nothing in the paper, describes the flag.

## D132. A `let` defers only the type of a call the pre-pass does not erase (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from a fault this lane found while hunting D130). In an untyped run (a called body, a sealed program's re-run) a `let` whose right-hand side is a call defers the call's type to its binding, computed if the binding's type is read (D82). A call that returns a type or a proof is erased: `evalAt` runs it as a statement on a private copy of the state (`inStatement`). The call deferred its type and wrote the record of it (`callTyLater`) inside the copy, `restoreKeep` discarded the record with the copy, and the binding had neither a type nor a deferred type, against D82's "every binding has its declared type". So an accepted function could not be called (the class of D119, D125, D129):
```
def TF (n : Nat) : Type := Nat
def useT (n : Nat) : Nat := (let T = TF(n); let z : T = 3; z)     -- accepted
def run3 : Eq(Nat, useT(1), 3) := refl                              -- rejected: cannot infer the type of the place T
```
and a closure capturing such a binding hit "INTERNAL [Capture] X has no stored type (D82: every binding has its declared type; please report)".
- **Rule.** A `let` defers its right-hand side's type exactly when the run is untyped, the right-hand side is a call, and the pre-pass does not erase it (`preFlags`, from its declared reading: neither a proof nor a sort; the decision `evalAt` makes for it). The call then defers when its type is wanted (the `let` always wants it) and its declared result does not read as a borrow (`callFn`). An erased call's type is computed inside its statement and comes back with its value, as a call's that is not deferred. Carrying the record out of the copy was refused: its argument values may hold loans the copy made.
- **The class, hunted** (kept as the asserted block). Right-hand sides: a call returning a type (`TF(n)`), one that closes off at an unknown argument (`TS(n)` with `TS` a match on `n`), a call of a closure (bound before the `let`, and inside the right-hand side, where the right-hand side is a `let` and so not a bare call), a proposition (`PF(n)`), a function type (`FT(n)`), a proof (`lem(n) : Eq(Nat, n, n)`, `lemZ(n) : IsZ(n)`); controls that are not a bare call (`((); TF(n))`, `(TF(n) : Type)`, `((); lem(n))`). Uses of the binding: an annotation (`let z : X = 3`), a Π-type over it, the tail, a capture, a call's argument, `clone`, a copy, a field with its parameter written, `J`, a match, an ascription. Each a generic function `G(n)`, a closed call of it by `refl` at `n = 1`, and a generic caller `H(n) := G(n)`, whose run reaches `TS(n)` at an unknown `n`. On main 24 closed calls and callers were rejected (the type, the closure call bound first, the closing-off call, the proposition and the function type, under an annotation, a Π-type, the tail or a capture); all are accepted now. The proof right-hand sides were accepted on main, since no use reads a proof binding's type in a run, and still are; their bindings now have types.
- **Other things a private copy discards that the run expects after it.** The machine passes two results back through the state rather than the return value: the erasure flags (`lastErased`, `lastProof`), which `evalAt` carries out of an erased term's copy explicitly, and this record. Everything else `restoreKeep` drops is the run's effects (the environment, the move log `placeLog`, the refinements `refs`, refinements of older unknowns' types), which a statement must not leave, or flags each evaluation sets on entry. It keeps the fresh names and the records that outlive a copy by design (`nextAbs`, `nextLoan`, `nextRecUid`, the new unknowns' types and frozen readings, `neutrals`, `etaRefs`, the recursion candidates).
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `Functions.ErasedLets` (216 rows, generated by `probes/erased-lets/gen.py`, all accepted; `TS`'s annotated uses left out, since `let z : TS(n) = 3` is rejected at the generic `n`). Mutation-tested: a flipped row and a wrong value each fail the build. Count 5,034 → 5,250.
  - Dump, main (df0ae549) with the new tests copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated and located over all 5,250 rows: 24 rows change, all in the new block, from rejected to accepted. Nothing else changes.
  - `lake exe sweep`: no new kind of violation; the known clause "the type of an unknown holds an unknown" gains one declaration now accepted, `ErasedLets.HTSPi` (the closed-off `TS(σ0)` is an unknown's type), 686 → 687; `--probes` identical; `--pushes` census identical and clean. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output.
  - Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, one core, interleaved, three runs each): none measurable (`Quicksort` 81.936G → 81.938G, `HashMapTests` 28.939G both, `Arrays` 1.9847G both).
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES (§2, "Every binding has its declared type", and the history line) says so; nothing in the paper describes the deferral.

## D133. An inductive type's own name stands in its fields only, not in its parameters' types (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof's machine lane). D118's check that a declaration names only declared things (`undeclaredCheck`) let an inductive declaration's own name through in its parameters' types as well as in its fields, although RULES and the paper already said "an inductive type's own name in its fields aside". So
```
inductive Q6 (A : Type) (x : (let z = Z; match z { Z => A, S(k) => Q6 })) := MkQ6
```
was accepted, its own name in a dead arm of a parameter's type, where the type does not yet exist.
- **Rule.** In a parameter's type the declaration's own name is not declared: `checkInd` checks the parameters' types with no own name allowed, and the fields' types with it, still before anything runs. The message is "[D133] Q6 is not declared in its own parameters' types: an inductive type's own name may stand in its fields only". RULES and the paper's text are unchanged: they already stated this; the checker now does it.
- **The class, hunted** (each position of the own name in a parameter's type). Accepted on main: in a dead arm, bare (`Q6`) and applied (`Q6a(A, A)`); under a `let` (`let T = match z { …, S(k) => QL }; T`); in an earlier parameter's type, a later one following (`QE`); inside a `Π` (`QP`); as a type function's argument (`TFid(QT)`); inside an equation (`Eq(Type, Nat, …QQ…)`); inside another inductive's argument (`Box(QBx)`). Rejected on main for their arity: written live with fewer arguments (`QD : (x : QD)`, `QDa`, `QPD` with `Box(QPD(A))`, and the suite's `InductiveModel.PD`, whose message changes). Written live with all its arguments, it made the checker run away (`Q3 (A : Type) (n : Nat) (x : Q3(A, n, n))`, `Q3b (A : Type) (x : Q3b(A, A))`). All are now rejected by D133, at once.
- **What the runaway was (a second finding, about the fuel budget, not changed here).** Evaluating `Q3`'s parameter type `Q3(A, n, n)` (typed, `evalTInd`) checks its arguments against `Q3`'s parameter types (`checkParams`), which evaluates `Q3(A, n, n)` again: an unbounded recursion through `evalTInd` → `checkParams` → `evalType`. Fuel bounds it: a direct run of `checkInd` on `Q3` ends "undecided: out of fuel" at every fuel tried. But each level keeps a copy of the state (`checkParams` runs `privately`, which saves the state, then pushes a frame onto the saved, shared environment array, which is copied), so memory grows with the square of the depth: 1.2G at fuel 50,000 (1.2 s), 2.7G at fuel 100,000 (6.3 s), more than 6G at fuel 200,000 (killed). At the default fuel, 2,000,000, the run exhausts the machine's memory long before its fuel. So the fuel budget bounds time but not memory: a recursion that grows the state at each level runs out of memory first. D133 closes this route: a parameter's type now names only inductives declared before it, so `checkParams`' recursion follows the declaration order and ends. The budget itself still bounds no memory.
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `Functions.OwnName` (18 rows: each position rejected with its message, `Q3` and `Q3b` among them; the own name in the fields, `QF` and `QFB`, and another type's name in a parameter's type, `QO`, accepted; another undeclared name keeps D118's message, `QU`), mutation-tested (a flipped row and a wrong message each fail the build). `InductiveModel.PD`'s asserted message updated. Count 5,250 → 5,268.
  - Dump, main (639251f6) with the new tests copied in (without `Q3` and `Q3b`, which run away there) and softened, against this change: unlocated and located: 8 rows of the new block from accepted to rejected, 3 rejected rows of it and `InductiveModel.PD` with the D133 message instead of the arity one, `Q3`, `Q3b` rejected (not run on main). Nothing else changes.
  - `lake exe sweep`: no new violation; the two known clauses lose the 3 declarations now rejected; `--probes`, `--pushes` identical. The probe files under `probes/` (25): identical output. Cost (`cpu_core`): none measurable.
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES' history line and D118's message list say so.

## D131. A binding's declared reading agrees with its type and its value (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof's machine lane: `origin/spine-machine12`, `Meaning/Steps/Machine/FitProofCE.lean`, and `reports/machine11.md`). With `PropF (n : Nat) : Type₁ := Prop`,
```
def V1 (k : Nat) : Unit := (let y = J(Nat, Z, Z, λ(x : Nat) : PropF(x) => ⊤, refl, refl); match k { Z => (let z = y; ()), S(j) => (), }; let w = y; ())
def V2 : Unit := V1(Z)
```
`V1` was accepted and `V2` rejected ("[Read] y was moved out … (reading ⊥)"); with the motive's codomain written `Prop` both are. The value of the `J` is a proof (its type `⊤` is a proposition), but the declared-type readers see a sort only where one is written (D55): `typeFnDecl` reads only a Π-type or data through a type function's body, so the motive reads as a function to data, `jErased` says no, and the `J` reads as data. A read never moves `⋆`, but the join decides its moves from readings, so at the instance it dropped `y`: the class of D119, D125, D129. The general fault: a binding's declared reading and the class of its type (or of its value) can disagree, and every decision that consumes the reading then disagrees with the run.
- **Rule (a safety net in the typed check).** At every binding a typed run makes, the reading's class agrees with the class of the stored type and with the class of the value: the class the declared reading gives the binding (a proof; a type; data) is the class of its stored type (a proposition; a sort; anything else), a binding holding a proof value `⋆` is read as a proof, and a binding read as a proof holds `⋆` or a stand-in whose content is not known yet (an unknown `σ`, at a proposition the run cannot evaluate; a sealed program). A disagreement is rejected: "[D131] y is a proof (its type ⊤ is a proposition), but its declared type reads as data: a sort is hidden from the reading, behind a call of a function whose body is a sort, a constant, an ascription or a match whose arms disagree; write the sort itself there" (or "y holds a proof (⋆, its type …)", "y is read as a proof, but holds …"). The bindings: the `let` of an evaluation (typed), `checkTailAt`'s `let`, and `checkFix`'s parameters (a function being checked and every λ). An unknown reading (a hole's) is not tested. The value is compared as well as the type because a stored type can be stuck on a hidden sort (an unknown's or a sealed type, classed as data, like the reading) while the value is already `⋆`. Not tested, and why: untyped runs (a callee's body, a sealed program's re-run: the generic typed check of the code they run makes the same bindings); the checker's own scaffolding (`callType`, `paramTypes`, `convPi`, `convFnRun`: they compute types, nothing reads their bindings' classes after); a capture (it copies a binding already tested, with its reading); a match's pattern variables (field places, whose types are first-order data, D36, and hold no hidden sort).
- **Not done (ruled to wait until a program wants it):** reading a type function's call whose body reads as a sort as that sort (`typeFnDecl`'s missing third case). With the net in place it is a pure widening; it would win back only the hunt's capture rows written with a call.
- **The class, hunted** (`scratchpad/checker9/gen131.py`, kept as the asserted block). Hidden sorts: `PropF(x)`, `(Prop : Type₁)`, a constant `PC : Type₁ := Prop`, `((); PropF(x))`, `(let z = 0; PropF(x))`, `match x { Z => Prop, S(m) => PropF(m) }`; the control `Prop`. Binders: a `let` of a `J` through a motive with that codomain (a proof read as data), the same captured by a closure, a `let` annotated with the hidden sort and a `let` of a call returning at it (a type read as data), a parameter and a λ's parameter at it, and a `J` whose type is stuck on the hidden sort at an unknown endpoint while its value is `⋆` (read in a match, captured, read twice). Each a generic function with `V1`'s shape, its instance and a generic caller. On main: the `J` rows (concrete and stuck type) were accepted with their instances rejected (the fault, 11 forms); the capture and read-twice rows were accepted with their instances (17 forms); the type rows were rejected because the first read moved the type (22 rows). Now every hidden form is rejected at the generic function by D131 (the stuck ones by the value test), and every control is accepted with its instance and caller.
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `Universes.HiddenSorts` (88 rows, generated by `probes/hidden-sorts/gen.py`, every rejection's message asserted, `V1` among them), mutation-tested (a flipped row and a wrong message each fail the build). Count 5,268 → 5,356.
  - Dump, main (d57e5ac8, and d5e9d2fa with the block copied in, softened) against this change: unlocated and located, without the block: all 5,268 rows identical, messages included; with it: 51 rows of the block change (29 accepted → rejected, 22 rejected with D131's message), nothing else. No program of the suite or the case studies is lost.
  - `lake exe sweep`: identical without the block; with it, the known clauses lose the declarations now rejected; `--probes`, `--pushes` identical; the 25 probe files identical.
  - Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, one core, interleaved, three runs each): `Quicksort` 81.939G → 81.955G (+0.02%), `HashMapTests` 28.940G → 28.950G (+0.03%), `Arrays` 1.9860G → 1.9883G (+0.11%).
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES (§2, after "Every binding has its declared type", and the history line) and the appendix's paragraph on declared proofs say so: "By [Type-pos] a type's declared sort is its computed sort, so a term is a proof exactly when its type is a proposition", which the fault falsified, is now "… and the typed check rejects a binding (a `let`, a parameter) whose declared type's class (a proof, a type, data) is not that of its type (a proposition, a sort, anything else) and of its value (`⋆` is a proof): a sort hidden behind a call, as `PropF(x)` with `PropF(n) : Type₁ := Prop`, made a `J` a proof read as data. So a binding is a proof exactly when its type is a proposition." The Conclusion ends on page 25.

## D134. A deferred call's head type is tested when the type is recorded (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker9]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof's machine lane: `origin/spine-machine13`, `Meaning/Steps/Machine/KindCE.lean`, `callAt stG`). In an untyped run, `callFn` computes a call's type, when it is wanted, with `declCallType`, which rejects a head type that is not a function type written as one ("not a function type") and a wrong arity. When a `let` is bound to the call it defers the type (D82, D132): the deferred path recorded `(piTy, ws)` in `callTyLater` untested. So a `let` of a call whose head's stored type is not a function type went on with a record nobody could use, where the same call outside a `let` was rejected. The head type is `funType`'s, the caller's stored type for the head, while `declKind` reads the value's own type, so nothing else rejected it. The proof lane's instance, at a hand-built state: `f ↦ g` stored at `Nat`, the untyped `f(Z)` with its type wanted and the flag set returned `Z` with no type and the record `(Nat, [Z])`.
- **Rule.** Deferral postpones computing a type, not the test that there is one: the deferred path makes the eager path's test when it records. One function makes it for both (`callPi`: the shape `.tPi _ (.pi hs ds c) _` and `ds.length = ws.size`, with `declCallType`'s messages, "not a function type: T" and "arity: m parameters, n arguments"), so the two paths cannot drift.
- **The hunt for a surface program** (`scratchpad/checker9/H134.lean`): a parameter whose type a split refines (`f : FT(n)`, `FT` a match on `n` returning a Π-type), a function behind a type function's result (`MkF(n) : FT(n)`), a sealed function (a match on an unknown returning functions), an unknown at a stuck type-function type called after a split; each called inside a `let` and outside one, at the generic call, a closed instance and a generic caller. Every one is accepted alike on main and after the change: no surface program reaches the difference. So the test is pinned on a state (`Units.lean`, `callKind`, as `KindCE.stG`): `f ↦ Add` stored at `Nat`, the call `f(0, 0)` is rejected with "not a function type: Nat" on both paths (before: the deferred path returned `0` with the record `(Nat, [0, 0])`); stored at a Π-type of one parameter, "arity: 1 parameters, 2 arguments" on both paths (before: the deferred path recorded it); at its own type the deferred path still records it. The two changed guards fail against main's checker.
- **Evidence.** No verdict changes, as expected (the proof lane's sweep finds every record a Π written as one, 70,742 states): the full dump against main (f58c7487), unlocated and located over 5,356 rows, identical; `lake exe sweep`, `--probes`, `--pushes` identical; the 25 probe files identical. Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, interleaved): `Quicksort` +0.005%, `HashMapTests` +0.01%, `Arrays` +0.03%. The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES' history line and its paragraph on deferred types say so; nothing in the paper describes the deferral.

## D135. While a function is being checked, its body is never run: a call of it closes off at once (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-22) [lane: checker10]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof lane: `origin/spine`, docs/31 D200). **The property, in one sentence: the body of a function whose check is under way (its entry on the [Rec] stack) is never run; a call of it closes off at once, as a call with a neutral head does, and the body runs only once [Def] has accepted the definition.** Lean does the same: the function being defined does not unfold inside its own body.
- **The gap.** `callFn` runs [Rec]'s test (`recCheck`) only in a typed run. A typed recursive call that passed it then ran the body in place, untyped: code the check had not finished with, whose calls no test saw and whose unchecked arms ran. With the `Nat` prelude and `inductive BB := TT | FF`, `def Loop (p : BB) (n : Nat) : Nat by n := match p { TT => match n { Z => Z, S(m) => Loop(FF, m), }, FF => Loop(FF, n), }` ended "undecided (call depth exceeded)": `Loop(FF, m)` passed the test, its in-place run called `Loop(FF, m)` untested, for ever, before the check reached the arm that is wrong. `HAt` made an untested call (`HAt(TT, 3)`, above its entry) before being rejected. No wrong acceptance is known, but the consistency proof cannot follow such a run: for a function still under check it knows only the induction hypothesis (some function good on smaller arguments), which says nothing about what the body computes (D200).
- **Why not the other ruling.** Ruled first and withdrawn the same hour: make [Rec]'s test at every call, typed or not. The test compares the argument with the OUTER definition's entry value, not with the running activation's own parameter, so `Loop` and `H2` still pass it in place and still loop (measured on a probe build).
- **Rule and code.** `underCheck fv` (Machine.lean): some [Rec] frame's function has the same code as `fv`. It is used once, in `callFn`, the only caller of `runBody` (the only place a body is run), where a neutral head closes off: `if head then stuckNow else closeCall` ([App-neutral], [App-neutral-head]). Two parts of it came from the hunt, each closing a hole the brief's probe patch (the stack searched with `recFrameOf`: the call's binding mark, else value equality) left open:
  - *By code, not by value* (D78's class, in a new place). A refinement or an [End] substitutes inside a local function's captures, and inside the captured proofs' types that `capture` writes into its code as `(⋆ : T)`, but not inside the [Rec] frame. The routes that reach a call of the function other than by its marked name see its value only: a sealed program's head call re-run after a split, a λ's capture of it run untyped (`runBodyCore` marks no binding), the typing of a sealed program. So `Value.sameCode` compares a global by its name and a closure by its `fix` term with every embedded value counted equal (`Term.sameCode`, beside `Term.beq` in Syntax.lean). With value equality, `CapSel`, `CapBlkSel`, `CapLamSel`, `CapPrf` were accepted (the body of `go` ran during `go`'s check) and `CapRe`, `CapLam`, `Nest` ended undecided; with the code compared by `==`, embedded values included, `CapPrf` was still accepted (a variant build). A false positive (an accepted closure whose code equals that of one being checked) can only close off a call that could have run: precision, never soundness.
  - *`sealedType?` keeps the frames.* It typed a sealed program by a typed run from the empty state with the [Rec] stack emptied, so a sealed program headed by the function being checked ran its body there: calling a sealed function value reads its own Π-type that way (`callFn`'s `funType? fv none`), and `FR`, a function returning a function, ended undecided under the probe patch. The frames now stay, with [Rec]'s test suspended (`RecCtx.tests := false`): the test applies there exactly as before; only "do not run" survives.
  - `recCheck`'s comment ("fail at the offending call, before it is run: running it may not terminate") is replaced: the call is not run.
- **The class, hunted** (the asserted block `Recursion.UnderCheck`, 45 rows; table in `scratchpad/reports/checker10.md`). Routes: a typed call run in place (`HAt`, `H2`, `Loop`); a sealed head call re-run after a later split, of a global (`ReRun`) and of a local function with the split on its argument (`CapSel0`), on its capture (`CapSel`, `CapRe`) or on what a captured proof's type mentions (`CapPrf`); a stuck block's re-run (`BlkRe`, `BlkSel`, `CapBlkSel`); a λ's capture, the λ called (`LamCall`) or passed to a complete higher-order function (`HoCall`), and of a local function whose capture was refined (`CapLam`); an inner recursive function, its own calls during its check (`InLoop`), its calls of the outer one during its check (`OutIn`) and after it (`OutIn2`: the inner one is then accepted code and runs, which is right, since every call it makes of the outer one closes off, so it runs no unchecked code), the outer one local with its capture refined (`Nest`); conversion of function values (`ConvFn`, `ConvLoop`); a borrow in the decreasing position (`BLoop`); erased calls: a proof-class call already returned without running (`PLoop`, unchanged), but a TYPE-class call ran its body on a private copy (`TLoop`, undecided on main); a sealed value's type (`STy`, `FR`). `split` on a call of the function being checked cannot be written (`split` names a global constant, and the function being defined is not in Σ). Every looping row is now rejected by [Rec] at the call it should be; the accepted controls stay accepted (`Ok` and `OkRuns`: a well-founded definition computes once accepted; `InnerRuns`: an inner function, once its own check has ended, computes inside the outer one's; `STyOk`, `FROk`).
- **The claim the typed-only test rests on**, tested: every call of a function being checked that no [Rec] test sees (an untyped call, or a typed one inside `sealedType?`) is an instance of a call site the typed check tested. An instrumented build ran the test at every such call and rejected with a marker where it failed: no marker over the suite's 5,356 rows and the hunt's 45 (and the dump otherwise identical). One finding on the way: `refine` re-runs the sealed programs a refinement reaches before recording it in `refs`, so a test made inside such a re-run reads the entry value without the refinement being made (the first instrumented run flagged `ReSel(0)` for that reason only; recording first, in the instrumented build, removed every flag).
- **THE LOSS**, of one kind: a body that needs its own recursive call to compute. `def Sel (n : Nat) : Nat by n := match n { Z => Z, S(m) => match m { Z => (let e : Eq(Nat, Sel(m), Z) = refl; Z), S(k) => Z, }, }` was accepted and is now rejected: "the ascribed term has type ⊤, expected Eq(Nat, ⌈Sel(0)⌉, 0)". The same equation stated after the definition holds (`Sel2After`). Fifteen rows of the hunt are of this kind (through a sealed re-run, a block, a λ, conversion, a borrow, an inner function, and at the type level, `TSel`: `let x : TSel(m) = Z` with `TSel(Z) = Nat`). Nothing in the suite or the case studies does this. The message is left as it is: it is produced by conversion, at whichever rule compares, far from the point where the call closes off, and closing off reports nothing a user sees; the sealed call `⌈Sel(0)⌉` in the expected type is the visible sign.
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted block `Recursion.UnderCheck` (45 rows, every rejection's message pinned), mutation-tested (a flipped row and a wrong message each fail the build). Count 5,356 → 5,401.
  - Dump, main (10502163) with the block copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated, all 5,356 rows of the suite and the case studies identical, messages included; 31 rows of the block change (15 accepted → rejected, the loss; 16 undecided → rejected by [Rec]). Located: the same, and 4 more rows of the block (`HAt`, `H2`, `Ok`, `Sel2`) differ only in their count of editor notes.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every difference (states checked, premises, the planted clause's violations) is the new block's own (each count's change equals the change of a sweep of `UnderCheck` alone); no clause gains a violation. `--probes`, `--pushes` identical. The 25 probe files under `probes/` identical.
  - Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, one core (10), interleaved, three runs each): `Quicksort` 81.964G → 81.984G (+0.02%), `HashMapTests` 28.953G → 28.884G (−0.24%), `Arrays` 1.9877G → 1.9762G (−0.58%).
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES ([Call], [Rec], the history line) and the paper (the caption of the typing figure; the appendix's [App-neutral], [T-Call] and [Rec] paragraphs and its table of side conditions) say so.

## D136. A local function is not recursive: recursion is for top-level definitions (orchestrator, user to acknowledge, 2026-11-27) [lane: checker11]
Decision (the orchestrator, from the proof lane; docs/32, "A LANGUAGE CHANGE the user may veto: a local function is not recursive"). **The property, in one sentence: only a top-level definition is recursive; a local function (a `fix` formed inside a body or a type) that declares `by x` is rejected, and a recursive helper is written as a top-level function taking what it used from the enclosing body as parameters.** A local function without `by` already could not name itself (D31), so no local function calls itself. A λ inside a recursive top-level definition still calls that definition, recognised by D78's marks.
- **Why.** The consistency proof's model treats the function under check as "some function good on smaller arguments" for top-level definitions only; a local recursive function has no counterpart there, and simulating its own check would need the very membership being proved. Lean's kernel has no local recursion either: its elaborator lifts `let rec` and `where` to top-level definitions with their captures as parameters, and Ochr can get the feature back the same way, as surface sugar the checker never sees. The feature had cost a closed proof of `False` (D78: a local recursive function whose capture a split refined), D78's marks, D135's recognition of a function by its code, and an objection from the theory's first referee; nothing outside the tests of those rules used it.
- **Rule and code.** Stated once, in `checkFix`, where every function's check starts: a function value that is not a global (`.gfn`) declaring `by` is rejected, "[Rec] go declares `by y`, but a local function is not recursive (D136): recursion is for top-level definitions. Define go at the top level, with what it uses from the enclosing body as parameters". Every local function is checked there (`evalCore`'s `.fix` in a typed run), in a function's or a constant's body, inside or around another local function, passed to a higher-order function, and in every type position (a codomain, a parameter's type, a `let`'s annotation, the sides of `Eq` and `Id`, a Π-type: types are evaluated by typed runs).
- **The hunt** (every way a local function could still recurse once `by` is rejected on it; table in `scratchpad/reports/checker11.md`; the programs are the asserted block `Recursion.LocalRec`). Through a binding of itself (a call, a nested λ, a stuck block's arm, a λ passed to a higher-order function, an assignment to it, its own signature): rejected by D31 (in its signature, "unknown constant"), on main as now. Through an inner or outer local function: an inner one calling a `by`-less outer one is D31 again; with `by` on either, the rule. Through a sealed program's or a stuck block's re-run, or a comparison of function values: each runs a local function only after its check has ended, since the program, the block or the closure is made by a call or a formation that needs the function's name or a value equal to it, which no code inside its own check has (a value equal to a closure contains its captures, so it would contain itself). A knot through a mutable place: a closure captures no borrow (`Knot1`), and a captured function is a snapshot, so reassigning the place it came from ties no knot (`Knot2` computes `0`). No route was found.
- **The deletion (its own commit, to be reverted with the rule).** The rule alone made nothing dead: a local function still got a [Rec] entry (with no decreasing position), and through it D135's recognition by code stayed reachable, as a false positive only: a λ being checked that calls an accepted closure of the same code took that closure for itself and closed the call off (`SameCode`: `l = λ(y). let e : Eq(Nat, κ1(clone(y)), y) = refl; y` over `k`, a closure of the same text over `k0`; rejected on main and under the rule alone, though `k` is accepted code). So the deletion begins with one change that is not a deletion: a local function gets no [Rec] entry, as the paper's [Rec] already said (its stack holds the functions being checked that declare `by`). A local entry could match a call only through the function's binding of itself (unreachable, D31) or a value equal to it (unreachable, above), and its other use, D135's "never run", met only closures of the same code whose own check had ended. Measured on an instrumented build of the rule alone (a marker wherever a [Rec] test matched a local entry, a call matched an entry without a decreasing position, a call's D78 mark held without its value, or D135's recognition matched a local entry), over the suite and the case studies (5,460 rows), the hunts of D135 and D136 and the 25 probe files: one marker, D135's at `SameCode`. With local entries gone, deleted as dead: `Value.sameCode`, `Term.sameCode` and its two helpers (Syntax.lean; `Scratch/Reach.lean` reaches 7,596 constants instead of 7,603, the seven gone being exactly those and their compiled helpers, and lists nothing new the kernel cannot see into); `underCheck` compares names; `recCheck`'s clause for a call of a function that declares no `by`. `SameCode` is accepted now, and is the one verdict the deletion changes.
- **Kept, and why.** D78's marks: every entry is now a top-level definition's, whose value is its name, so a call through a marked binding always has that value (the instrumented build: no call had the mark without the value), and the marks decide nothing that comparing names would not; kept as the decision read from syntax for a λ inside a recursive top-level definition. The candidates accumulator (`RecCtx.cands`, and `keepCands` in `restore` and `restoreKeep`): constant since D23 (a frame's candidates are its one declared position, and a call that removes it rejects), so not made dead by D136; left as it is.
- **Not covered.** The rule, like D31, holds where a function's check starts, so a local function in an arm no run takes (a match on a constructor takes one arm, typed or not) is not held to it: `let z = Z; match z { Z => x, S(k) => (fix go (y : Nat) : Nat by y := go(y))(k) }` is accepted, as the same arm with a `by`-less `go` calling itself is. No run reaches such code. A syntactic check before anything runs, as D118 makes for names, would hold it; not done.
- **THE LOSS.** A recursive helper can no longer close over its surroundings: it takes them as arguments. Five accepted rows of the suite, all in `Examples/Recursion.lean`: `Outer`, `OuterBadOk`, `CapSplitOk`, `CapSplitLamOk` and `InnerRuns`, each now beside its lifted form, accepted (`OuterLift`, `OuterBadOkLift`, `CapSplitOkLift`, `CapSplitLamOkLift` with a closed run, `InnerRunsLift`). Every lost program of the hunt lifts too. One kind needs more than moving the captures to parameters: a helper that calls the enclosing top-level definition back (`TopIn`, `Twice`), since top-level definitions are not mutually recursive (Lean lifts such a `let rec` into a mutual block). It takes that call as a function argument, a λ inside the definition, whose call [Rec] tests against the definition's entry (`TopInLift`), or the call's value when it makes the call more than once on a path (`TwiceLift`: a data closure is not a copy). The lifted forms of D78's proofs of `False` are rejected by [Rec], as before (`CapSplitGo`, `CapSplitLamGo`).
- **Evidence.**
  - Asserted rows: `Recursion` (12 new: the lifted forms, with the changed rows' messages pinned), `UnderCheck` (2 new; its 13 rows on a local recursive function now pinned to D136's message), and the new block `LocalRec` (47 rows: the hunt's programs, their lifted forms and closed runs, `SameCode`), every rejection's message pinned; mutation-tested (a flipped row and a wrong message each fail the build). Count 5,401 → 5,462.
  - Dump, main (08ff29a95) with the new tests copied in and softened, against this change: unlocated and located, every row of the suite and the case studies outside these three blocks identical, messages included; 37 rows of the blocks change: 16 accepted → rejected by D136 (the five suite rows above, and the hunt's `LocConst`, `InBy`, `HoLoc`, `TopIn`, `Twice` and six type positions), 19 rejected with D136's message instead of [Rec]'s or a conversion's, `SameCode` and `SameCodeRuns` rejected → accepted. The rule's commit alone against both commits: `SameCode` and `SameCodeRuns` only.
  - `lake exe sweep`: every difference is the three blocks' own (each count's change equals the change of a sweep of those blocks alone); no clause gains a violation. `--probes` identical; `--pushes`: no temporary pushed without a stored type. The 25 probe files under `probes/` identical.
  - Cost (instructions retired on `cpu_core`, one core (10), interleaved, three runs each): `Quicksort` 81.986G → 81.942G (−0.05%), `HashMapTests` 28.882G → 28.844G (−0.13%), `Arrays` 1.9763G → 1.9714G (−0.25%).
  - The build is green with no new `sorry`. RULES (the grammar's `fix`, [Call], [Rec], the history line) and the paper (the syntax section and its figure's caption, the discussion's loops, the appendix's grammar, [App-neutral], [Rec], [Def] and its table of side conditions) say so; the Conclusion ends on page 25.
- **Amended (2026-11-27, lane checker12): every function's binding of itself is marked again.** The deletion commit (36e88d7c) made `checkFix` give its binding of itself a mark (`Binding.recUid`, a fresh `nextRecUid`, `markLastRec`) only for a function that declares `by`, since the checker reads no other function's mark: no [Rec] entry carries it, and the function's name is not in scope in its body (D31). The consistency proof reads it: its relation finds the binding of a body's frame that is the function itself by the mark, and eight of the proof's checks of recorded states failed when D136 was merged into the proof line. The mark states a fact about the state (this binding is the function's own), whether or not the function recurses. **Rule:** `checkFix` marks every function's binding of itself with a fresh mark, as before 36e88d7c (the same numbering); only a function with `by` gets an entry on the [Rec] stack, carrying that mark (that part of D136 stands).
